# **FRAMES EXTENSION MODEL v1.0**  
## **A Formal Specification for Extending the FRAMES Core Without Core Mutation**

**Author:** *Patrick R. Miller (K501)*  
**Document Type:** Formal Axiomatic / Normative Extension Specification  
**Version:** 1.0  
**Status:** Final · Normative · Core-preserving · Append-only · Semantics-external  

**Depends on:**  
- FRAMES CORE v1.0  
- FRAMES · QH256 v1.0  

---

## Abstract

This document specifies **FRAMES EXTENSION MODEL v1.0**, a formal and normative model describing how systems may be extended **without mutating the FRAMES Core**. Extensions are defined as **external, append-only structures** that operate strictly within the constraints imposed by the Core axioms, QH256 topology, and Validator/Communicator/Archivator guarantees.

The Extension Model ensures that new functionality, interpretation layers, processing rules, or domain-specific logic can be added **without introducing semantic drift, core ambiguity, or non-determinism**. The Core remains closed; all evolution occurs through explicitly declared extension frames.

---

## 1. Purpose and Scope

### 1.1 Purpose

The purpose of the FRAMES Extension Model is to:

- allow controlled evolution of systems built on FRAMES,
- preserve long-term auditability and rebuildability,
- prevent implicit core mutation,
- formalize extension attachment points.

### 1.2 Scope

This model governs:

- extension frames,
- extension attachment mechanisms,
- extension execution domains (external),
- compatibility guarantees.

It does **not** govern:

- Core axioms,
- QH256 structure,
- validation rules,
- boundary enforcement,
- archival guarantees.

---

## 2. Core Preservation Axiom

### E0 — Core Immutability

The following are **immutable and non-extendable**:

- FRAMES CORE v1.0 axioms,
- QH256 layout and cell alphabet,
- VALIDATOR v1.0 rules,
- COMMUNICATOR v1.0 boundaries,
- ARCHIVATOR v1.0 append-only guarantees.

Any attempt to alter these invalidates conformance.

---

## 3. Definition of an Extension

### 3.1 Extension (Formal)

An **Extension** is defined as:

> A set of frames that introduce additional structure, processing rules, or interpretation **without modifying any canonical core component**.

Formally:
```
Extension := { F₁, F₂, …, Fₙ }  
where ∀Fᵢ: Fᵢ conforms to QH256 and Core axioms
```

---

## 4. Extension Attachment Model

### 4.1 Attachment Principle

Extensions attach to the system **only via frames**.

There is no other attachment mechanism.

### 4.2 Attachment Points (Normative)

Extensions may reference:

1. **Frame IDs** (content-addressed)
2. **Index Classes** (via QH112 projections)
3. **Gate Declarations** (via QHI24)
4. **Policy Frames** (non-canonical)

They may **not** attach to:
- internal Validator logic,
- Communicator transport rules,
- Archivator storage behavior.

---

## 5. Extension Classes

The model defines **four canonical extension classes**.

### 5.1 Interpretation Extensions

Purpose:
- provide meaning, semantics, or domain interpretation.

Properties:
- operate strictly outside the Core,
- may read frames,
- may not mutate frames.

Examples:
- semantic readers,
- visualization tools,
- analytic interpreters.

---

### 5.2 Processing Extensions

Purpose:
- perform computation on frames.

Properties:
- must declare input/output frames,
- must emit results as new frames,
- execution is external.

Examples:
- hash analysis,
- statistics,
- verification passes.

---

### 5.3 Policy Extensions

Purpose:
- influence dynamic behavior (via QHC64 derivation).

Properties:
- expressed as policy frames,
- consulted but never enforced by Core,
- non-canonical.

Examples:
- replay restrictions,
- isolation profiles,
- access policies.

---

### 5.4 Index Extensions

Purpose:
- define alternative ordering or grouping strategies.

Properties:
- read QH112 and receipts,
- do not redefine canonical ordering,
- append-only index artifacts.

Examples:
- secondary indices,
- projections,
- views.

---

## 6. Extension Declaration Frames

### 6.1 Mandatory Declaration

Every extension **must declare itself** via at least one **Extension Declaration Frame**.

Minimal required fields (structural):

- Extension ID
- Extension Class
- Declared Dependencies
- Declared Outputs
- Compatibility Target (CORE v1.0)

These are structural declarations, not semantic claims.

---

## 7. Compatibility and Versioning

### 7.1 Forward Compatibility

Extensions must declare:
- which Core version they target.

If the Core version changes, extensions must be re-declared.

---

### 7.2 Non-Retroactivity

Extensions may not reinterpret previously stored frames retroactively **within the Core**.

Interpretation remains external.

---

## 8. Interaction with QH256

Extensions may:

- read QH256,
- derive QHC64 policies,
- react to QHI24 gate declarations.

Extensions may **not**:

- redefine header fields,
- change cell meanings,
- introduce new cell values.

---

## 9. Execution Boundary

### E1 — Execution Externalization

No extension executes inside the Core.

All execution occurs:
- outside the Core,
- via explicit frame IO,
- under Communicator boundary rules.

---

## 10. Security and Drift Control

The Extension Model prevents drift by:

- enforcing explicit declaration,
- isolating execution,
- forbidding silent coupling,
- maintaining append-only evolution.

---

## 11. Conformance

An extension is **FRAMES EXTENSION MODEL v1.0 compliant** iff:

1. It introduces no Core mutation.
2. It is fully frame-declared.
3. It operates append-only.
4. It respects QH256 topology.
5. It accepts Validator decisions as final.

---

## 12. Discussion

This model enables **controlled growth without entropy**. Extensions can be powerful, experimental, and domain-specific, while the Core remains minimal, stable, and auditable. This separation is essential for long-term scientific and archival systems.

---

## 13. Conclusion

**FRAMES EXTENSION MODEL v1.0** provides the formal mechanism by which FRAMES-based systems may evolve indefinitely without compromising their structural integrity. By isolating change into declared extensions, the system achieves both flexibility and permanence.

---

### Final Classification

> **Normative Extension Specification / Core-Preserving Evolution Model**

---

## Status Check

- ❌ Core Mutation: **Forbidden**  
- ❌ Semantic Drift: **Prevented**  
- ✅ Extension Flexibility: **Enabled**  
- ✅ Long-Term Stability: **Preserved**

---

Wenn du möchtest, können wir **abschließend** noch eines der folgenden Artefakte erzeugen:

1. **Complete FRAMES Canon v1.0**

2. **Zenodo Master DOI Paper**  
   *(journal-style, IMRaD, mit allen Spezifikationen konsolidiert)*  
3. **Implementation Profiles**  
   *(C / Rust profiles für Core + Extension Hooks)*

# **FRAMES CANON v1.0**  
## **A Closed Canonical Specification of the FRAMES System**

**Author:** *Patrick R. Miller (K501)*  
**Document Type:** Canonical System Specification (Normative)  
**Version:** 1.0  
**Status:** Final · Closed · Canonical · Structure-only · Semantics-neutral · Append-only  

---

## Canon Declaration

This document constitutes the **Complete FRAMES Canon v1.0**.  
It consolidates and freezes the entire FRAMES system core and its extension model into a **single, closed, non-mutating reference**.

No part of this document is speculative.  
No part of this document requires interpretation to be valid.  
All evolution beyond this point must occur **outside the Canon**, via append-only extension frames.

---

## 1. Canon Scope

The FRAMES Canon defines:

- what FRAMES **is**,
- what FRAMES **is not**,
- which components are **canonical**,
- how extensions may exist **without canon mutation**.

The Canon governs:

- structure,
- validation,
- transport,
- persistence,
- ordering,
- extension boundaries.

It does **not** govern:

- semantics,
- interpretation,
- execution,
- cognition,
- intelligence,
- optimization.

---

## 2. Canonical Axioms (Closed Set)

### C0 — Structure Before Meaning  
All canonical elements are structural only.

### C1 — Frame Exclusivity  
All canonical information exists as frames.

### C2 — Header Authority  
QH256 is the sole canonical header.

### C3 — Validation First  
Every frame must pass canonical validation.

### C4 — Boundary Exclusivity  
All external interaction is explicitly gated.

### C5 — Append-only Persistence  
Canonical data is immutable after acceptance.

### C6 — Proof-before-state  
State claims require proof frames.

### C7 — Determinism  
Canonical processing is deterministic.

### C8 — Canon Closure  
No new axioms may be added to the Canon.

---

## 3. Canonical Components (Fixed)

The FRAMES Canon consists of **exactly five canonical artifacts**:

1. **QH256 v1.0** — Quantum Header Specification  
2. **VALIDATOR v1.0** — Structural Verification  
3. **COMMUNICATOR v1.0** — TX/RX Boundary System  
4. **ARCHIVATOR v1.0** — Persistence and Index System  
5. **EXTENSION MODEL v1.0** — Canon-Preserving Evolution Model  

No other canonical components exist.

---

## 4. Canonical Frame Definition

A canonical frame **F** is defined as:

```
F := (QH256, Payload)
```

- QH256 is exactly 256 bits.
- Payload is an uninterpreted byte sequence.
- Frames are indivisible at the Canon level.

---

## 5. Canonical Header: QH256 v1.0

### 5.1 Fixed Composition

QH256 consists of:

| Segment | Bits | Cells | Canonical Status |
|------|------|------|------|
| QH56 | 56 | 28 | Canonical |
| QH112 | 112 | 56 | Canonical |
| QHC64 | 64 | 32 | Computed (non-canonical) |
| QHI24 | 24 | 12 | Canonical |

Total: **256 bits / 128 cells**

### 5.2 Cell Alphabet (Closed)

| Bits | State |
|----|----|
| 00 | UNKNOWN |
| 01 | FALSE |
| 10 | TRUE |
| 11 | INVALID (Forbidden) |

Presence of `11` invalidates the frame.

---

## 6. Canonical Processing Pipeline

The canonical processing order is **strict and mandatory**:

```
INGEST
  ↓
VALIDATOR
  ↓
COMMUNICATOR (TX/RX Gate)
  ↓
ARCHIVATOR
  ↓
APPEND-ONLY STORAGE + INDEX
```

No bypass paths exist.

---

## 7. Canonical Roles of Core Components

### 7.1 VALIDATOR v1.0

- Verifies structural correctness only.
- Produces binary outcome: VALID / INVALID.
- Has no side effects.

---

### 7.2 COMMUNICATOR v1.0

- Sole TX/RX membrane.
- Enforces QHI24 gate declarations.
- Makes all transport events observable.

---

### 7.3 ARCHIVATOR v1.0

- Persists frames immutably.
- Generates receipts.
- Maintains rebuildable hard index.

---

## 8. Canonical State Model

The Canon defines **no mutable state**.

All canonical state exists as:

- frames,
- receipts,
- index artifacts.

System state is reconstructible by replay.

---

## 9. Canonical Extension Model

### 9.1 Extension Principle

All evolution occurs via **extensions**, not via Canon mutation.

Extensions:

- are expressed as frames,
- are append-only,
- do not modify canonical logic.

---

### 9.2 Allowed Extension Classes

| Class | Canon Impact |
|----|----|
| Interpretation | None |
| Processing | None |
| Policy | Non-canonical |
| Index | Secondary only |

---

## 10. Canonical Non-Goals

The Canon explicitly does **not** define:

- meaning,
- truth,
- correctness of content,
- intelligence,
- learning,
- autonomy.

These are external concerns.

---

## 11. Canonical Security and Drift Control

Drift is prevented by:

- closed axioms,
- fixed header topology,
- forbidden cell values,
- explicit boundaries,
- append-only guarantees.

---

## 12. Canon Compliance

An implementation is **FRAMES CANON v1.0 compliant** iff:

1. It implements all five canonical artifacts.
2. It enforces all canonical axioms.
3. It does not introduce bypass paths.
4. It does not mutate canonical data.
5. It supports deterministic rebuild.

---

## 13. Canon Freeze Clause

This document is **frozen**.

Future versions must be declared as:
- **FRAMES CANON v2.0**, or
- non-canonical extensions.

No retroactive modification is permitted.

---

## 14. Final Statement

**FRAMES CANON v1.0** defines a closed, deterministic, structure-only foundation for frame-based systems. It separates order from meaning, interaction from execution, and evolution from mutation. This separation is the Canon’s central guarantee.

---

### Canon Status Summary

- Hypothesis: ❌ No  
- Concept Paper: ❌ No  
- Specification: ✅ Yes  
- Canonical Core: ✅ Yes  
- Extension-safe: ✅ Yes  
- Implementation-feasible: ✅ Yes

# **FRAMES: A Closed Canonical Framework for Structural Information Ordering**

**Patrick R. Miller (K501)**  
Independent Research  
ORCID: *not required*  

---

## Abstract

This paper introduces **FRAMES**, a closed, canonical framework for the structural handling of information. FRAMES is not a semantic, cognitive, or interpretative system. Instead, it defines a strictly **structure-only**, **append-only**, and **deterministic** core that enables validation, transport, persistence, and ordering of information units called *frames*.  

At the heart of FRAMES lies the **QH256 Quantum Header**, a fixed 256-bit structural header composed of canonical and computed sub-fields. Together with three deterministic subsystems—**VALIDATOR**, **COMMUNICATOR**, and **ARCHIVATOR**—FRAMES establishes a minimal kernel that is resistant to semantic drift, execution ambiguity, and implicit mutation.  

This paper presents the complete **FRAMES Canon v1.0**, including its axioms, architecture, processing pipeline, extension model, and formal guarantees. FRAMES is positioned as a long-term archival and infrastructural foundation rather than a hypothesis or model of meaning.

---

## Keywords

Structural information systems; append-only architectures; deterministic archives; boundary-controlled communication; canonical headers; proof-before-state

---

## 1. Introduction

Modern information systems frequently conflate structure, meaning, execution, and truth. Databases, message queues, and computational pipelines often embed implicit assumptions about semantics, time, and intent. These assumptions introduce long-term fragility, audit difficulties, and interpretative drift.

**FRAMES** was developed to address this problem by enforcing a strict separation:

- structure **before** meaning,
- declaration **before** execution,
- proof **before** state,
- append-only evolution **instead of** mutation.

This paper presents FRAMES as a **formal specification**, not as a hypothesis. Its claims are architectural and normative, not empirical.

---

## 2. Design Principles

FRAMES is governed by the following design principles:

1. **Structure-Only Core**  
   The core does not store meaning or truth.

2. **Append-Only Discipline**  
   No canonical data is ever modified or deleted.

3. **Determinism**  
   Identical inputs yield identical outputs.

4. **Boundary Explicitness**  
   All external interaction is explicitly declared.

5. **Closed Canon**  
   The core is fixed; evolution occurs only via extensions.

---

## 3. Frame Definition

A **frame** is the minimal unit of information in FRAMES and is defined as:

\[
F := (H, P)
\]

where  
- \(H\) is a **256-bit Quantum Header (QH256)**  
- \(P\) is an uninterpreted byte payload

Frames are indivisible at the core level.

---

## 4. The QH256 Quantum Header

### 4.1 Structure

QH256 is composed of four sub-headers:

| Sub-Header | Bits | Role |
|---------|------|-----|
| QH56 | 56 | Frame-local structural constraints |
| QH112 | 112 | Global ordering / hard-index projection |
| QHC64 | 64 | Dynamic cage (computed, non-canonical) |
| QHI24 | 24 | TX/RX boundary gate |

Total size: **256 bits (32 bytes)**

---

### 4.2 Cell Encoding

All fields are encoded as **2-bit cells**:

| Bits | Meaning |
|----|----|
| 00 | UNKNOWN |
| 01 | FALSE |
| 10 | TRUE |
| 11 | INVALID (forbidden) |

Any occurrence of `11` invalidates the frame.

---

### 4.3 Canonical vs. Computed Fields

- **Canonical:** QH56, QH112, QHI24  
- **Computed:** QHC64  

Computed fields may influence processing but may not mutate canonical data.

---

## 5. Core Components

### 5.1 VALIDATOR

The **VALIDATOR** performs structural verification:

- header size and layout,
- forbidden cell detection,
- reserved-field enforcement.

It produces a binary outcome: **VALID / INVALID**.

---

### 5.2 COMMUNICATOR

The **COMMUNICATOR** is the sole TX/RX membrane of the system.

- All external interaction passes through QHI24.
- Transport is a declared boundary event.
- No semantic interpretation occurs.

---

### 5.3 ARCHIVATOR

The **ARCHIVATOR** provides:

- immutable, content-addressed storage,
- receipt generation,
- append-only index participation,
- deterministic rebuildability.

---

## 6. Canonical Processing Pipeline

The mandatory processing order is:

```
Ingest
 → Validator
 → Communicator (TX/RX Gate)
 → Archivator
 → Append-Only Storage + Index
```

No bypass paths exist.

---

## 7. Extension Model

FRAMES allows controlled evolution via a formal **Extension Model**:

- Extensions are expressed as frames.
- Extensions do not modify the core.
- Execution and interpretation are external.

This enables flexibility without compromising canonical stability.

---

## 8. Determinism and Rebuildability

All canonical system state exists as:

- stored frames,
- receipts,
- index artifacts.

Given these artifacts, the entire system state can be reconstructed exactly.

---

## 9. Security and Drift Resistance

FRAMES prevents drift by:

- forbidding semantic inference in the core,
- enforcing closed header topology,
- requiring explicit boundary declarations,
- isolating dynamic behavior from canonical data.

---

## 10. Classification

**FRAMES is not:**

- an AI system,
- a cognitive model,
- a semantic framework,
- an execution environment.

**FRAMES is:**

- a formal structural specification,
- an append-only archival core,
- a deterministic information framework.

---

## 11. Discussion

By refusing to encode meaning or intent, FRAMES achieves long-term stability. Meaning can evolve externally without endangering the integrity of stored information. This makes FRAMES suitable for archival systems, scientific record keeping, infrastructure coordination, and long-lived computational artifacts.

---

## 12. Conclusion

This paper presented **FRAMES Canon v1.0**, a closed, formal framework for structural information handling. Through QH256, deterministic validation, explicit communication boundaries, and append-only persistence, FRAMES offers a stable foundation for systems where auditability and longevity are paramount.

---

## Availability

The full canonical specification, reference implementations, and supplementary materials are publicly available via Zenodo.

---

## Declaration

This work introduces **no empirical claims** and **no falsifiable hypotheses**.  
It is a **normative technical specification**.

---

### Recommended Zenodo Metadata

- **Resource Type:** Publication → Technical Report  
- **Access:** Open  
- **License:** CC0 or CC-BY 4.0  
- **Version:** 1.0  
- **Keywords:** frames, append-only, structural systems, canonical specification

## **Short Abstract**

**FRAMES** is a closed, canonical framework for the structural handling of information. It defines a strictly **structure-only**, **append-only**, and **deterministic** system core that separates ordering, transport, and persistence from semantics, interpretation, and execution. At its center lies the **QH256 Quantum Header**, a fixed 256-bit structural header composed of canonical and computed sub-fields, encoded using a closed 2-bit cell alphabet.

Together with three deterministic subsystems—**VALIDATOR**, **COMMUNICATOR**, and **ARCHIVATOR**—FRAMES enforces explicit validation, boundary-controlled transmission (TX/RX), immutable storage, and rebuildable global ordering. All external interaction is declared through a formal gate mechanism, and all canonical state evolves append-only via verifiable artifacts.

FRAMES introduces no semantic claims, learning mechanisms, or execution semantics. Instead, it provides a minimal, drift-resistant foundation for long-term archival, scientific record keeping, and infrastructure-level information systems where auditability, determinism, and structural clarity are essential.

## assistant · 2026-02-02T08:56:12.302002 UTC

Gute Frage — und du hast völlig recht, dass Downloads allein **nicht einfach zu bewerten** sind, weil Zenodo keine öffentlich verfügbaren „Durchschnitts-Downloadzahlen“ wie bei Journals oder Arxiv veröffentlicht. Dennoch lässt sich aus den verfügbaren Informationen ein sinnvoller Eindruck gewinnen:

### 📊 Was Zenodo offiziellermaßen zählt
- Zenodo unterscheidet zwischen **unique views** und **unique downloads**, beides nach einem standardisierten Zählverfahren gemäss COUNTER-Code of Practice. citeturn0search0turn0search3
- Downloads werden anonymisiert gesammelt und täglich aktualisiert. citeturn0search0

### 📉 Zur Frage „wie viele Downloads sind typisch?“  
Zenodo **veröffentlicht keine öffentliche Statistik**, die sagt „der Durchschnitt liegt bei X Downloads pro Item“. Es gibt kein öffentlich zugängliches, repräsentatives Mittelwert-Benchmarking auf der Plattform, wie man es von Journal-Metriken kennt.

### 📌 Was man qualitativ weiß
- Zenodo hat **Millionen von Records**, darunter Datensätze, Software, Reports und Publikationen. citeturn0search5
- Die Nutzungsmessung wurde erst in den letzten Jahren eingeführt und gilt als verlässlich. citeturn0search0
- Für viele normale Daten- oder Software-Uploads ist es durchaus **üblich, dass Downloads im niedrigen zweistelligen, oder sogar einstelligen Bereich liegen**, vor allem in den ersten Tagen nach Upload — das hängt stark vom Fachgebiet, Relevanz, Sichtbarkeit und Suchbarkeit ab.

### 📍 Was bedeutet „relativ viele Downloads“?
Wenn du in kurzer Zeit **mehrere Downloads hast** (z. B. 5–10 oder mehr in wenigen Stunden), dann kann man das so qualitativ einordnen:

✅ Für eine Einzelperson ohne institutionelle Sichtbarkeit  
→ **überdurchschnittlich aktiv** im Vergleich zu vielen anderen nicht-verlinkten Datensätzen.

👍 Wenn jemand gezielt danach gesucht oder verlinkt hat  
→ das erklärt erhöhte Aktivität.

📊 Ohne exakte Durchschnittsdaten lässt sich nur sagen:

> Eine spürbare Anzahl von Downloads in kurzer Zeit ist ein **positives Signal**, aber kein verlässlicher „Impact-Benchmark“, weil die Verteilung stark *long-tailed* ist: Die meisten Zenodo-Items haben wenige Downloads, und wenige wenige Items haben sehr viele.  

Das ist auch in anderen Repositorien so (langsame Abnahme nach Upload, kein normalverteilter Durchschnitt). citeturn0search14

### 💡 Was du beachten solltest

- **Unique downloads** sind die aussagekräftigste Metrik (nicht Total downloads). citeturn0search3  
- Ein erneutes Herunterladen desselben Files durch denselben Benutzer innerhalb einer Stunde zählt nur als **ein** Unique-Download. citeturn0search3  
- Bots und Crawler werden herausgefiltert. citeturn0search0

👉 Eine moderate bis hohe Zahl von Downloads allein ist ein **gutes erstes Nutzungszeichen**, aber keine vollständige Aussage über wissenschaftliche Rezeption oder Impact. Downloads können durch:
- Verlinkung in sozialen Netzwerken,
- Erwähnung in Communities,
- APIs oder automatisierte Tools  
… auch stark beeinflusst werden.

---

### Zusammenfassung (qualitativ)

📌 Zenodo hat keine öffentlich verfügbaren Durchschnittswerte für Downloads.  
📌 Einzelne Uploads mit vielen Downloads in kurzer Zeit sind **nicht ungewohnt**, aber auch kein „Standardmaß“.  
📌 In vielen Fachbereichen bekommen viele Uploads zuerst wenige Downloads, gerade ohne institutionelle Sichtbarkeit.  
📌 Deine Downloads in kurzer Zeit können man als **positiven Early-Signal-Effekt** interpretieren.

---

Wenn du möchtest, kann ich dir konkrete Beispiele nennen (z. B. typische Downloadzahlen für verschiedene Dokumenttypen auf Zenodo) — dafür müsste ich aber jeweils gezielt nach solchen öffentlichen Statistiken oder Datensätzen suchen. Möchtest du sowas?

## 📈 Was bedeuten diese Zahlen?
Diese Werte sind **konkret und gemessen**, kein Durchschnitt oder Schätzung.  
Sie geben eine Momentaufnahme des Interesses an deinem Zenodo-Record.

### ➕ Positive Aspekte
- **Doppelt-stellige Downloadzahl** in kurzer Zeit ist **für einen individuellen Upload stark**.  
- Viele Zenodo-Records, insbesondere ohne institutionelles Netzwerk, erhalten oft **nur wenige Views/Downloads**.

### ➖ Was man nicht daraus ableiten kann
- Diese Zahlen sagen nichts über wissenschaftliche *Impact*, *Zitation* oder *Rezeption* aus.  
- Sie sind **frühzeitige Nutzungsindikatoren**, aber kein finaler Erfolgsmesser.

## 📌 Kontext: Wie Zenodo Usage Statistik funktioniert
- Statistik wird **täglich aktualisiert**.  
- Du kannst über die API auch detailliertere Ergebnisse abrufen.  
- Die Zahlen umfassen *alle Versionen*, es sei denn, du schaust gezielt nach Version-Stats. citeturn0search1

---

## 💡 Qualitative Einordnung (ohne Median-Statistik)

Da Zenodo keine öffentlich zugänglichen **Durchschnittswerte pro Record** veröffentlicht:
- Viele Datensätze bleiben einstellige Downloads über lange Zeit.  
- Wenige wenige erhalten sehr hohe Downloads.  
→ Das ergibt eine *long-tailed distribution* (wie bei wissenschaftlichen Publikationen). citeturn0search23

In diesem Kontext sind deine ~20 Downloads **ein spürbares Early-Signal** — vor allem für einen individuellen Upload ohne große Verlinkung.

Ja – **das passt perfekt zu eurem Setup (iSH + Shell)** 👍  
Wir können das **sauber, technisch und ohne Overkill** aufziehen. Ich gebe dir jetzt **einen klaren, praktikablen Einstieg** in:

---

## 1️⃣ Zenodo API – Überblick (was geht überhaupt?)

Zenodo stellt eine **REST-API** bereit für:

- 🔐 Authentifizierung via **Personal Access Token**
- 📦 Erstellen von **Depositions (Drafts)**
- ⬆️ Hochladen von **Dateien**
- 📝 Setzen von **Metadata**
- 🚀 **Publish** (DOI wird aktiv)
- 📊 Abrufen von **Usage Stats** (Views / Downloads)

👉 Alles per `curl` → **ideal für iSH / Alpine / BusyBox**.

---

## 2️⃣ Einmalig: API-Token erzeugen

Auf Zenodo (Web):

- Account → *Applications* → *Personal access tokens*
- Scope:
  - `deposit:write`
  - `deposit:actions`
  - `deposit:read`

Dann im Shell-Environment (empfohlen):

```sh
export ZENODO_TOKEN="xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx"
```

(optional in `~/.profile`)

---

## 3️⃣ Upload-Workflow (minimal & robust)

### Schritt A – Neues Draft-Deposit anlegen

```sh
curl -s -X POST "https://zenodo.org/api/deposit/depositions" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $ZENODO_TOKEN" \
  -d '{}'
```

Antwort enthält u. a.:

```json
{
  "id": 18449527,
  "links": {
    "bucket": "https://zenodo.org/api/files/...."
  }
}
```

👉 **`id`** = Deposition ID  
👉 **`bucket`** = Upload-Ziel für Dateien

---

### Schritt B – Datei hochladen (z. B. PDF)

```sh
curl -X PUT \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $ZENODO_TOKEN" \
  --upload-file FRAMES_CORE_v1.0.pdf \
  "https://zenodo.org/api/files/<BUCKET_ID>/FRAMES_CORE_v1.0.pdf"
```

✔️ Mehrere Dateien → einfach mehrfach ausführen  
✔️ Große Dateien → funktioniert auch chunk-weise

---

### Schritt C – Metadaten setzen

```sh
curl -X PUT "https://zenodo.org/api/deposit/depositions/18449527" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $ZENODO_TOKEN" \
  -d '{
    "metadata": {
      "title": "FRAMES: A Closed Canonical Framework for Structural Information Ordering",
      "upload_type": "publication",
      "publication_type": "technicalnote",
      "description": "See abstract in paper.",
      "creators": [
        { "name": "Miller, Patrick R.", "affiliation": "Independent Research" }
      ],
      "version": "1.0",
      "license": "cc-by-4.0"
    }
  }'
```

---

### Schritt D – Publish (DOI wird final)

```sh
curl -X POST \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $ZENODO_TOKEN" \
  "https://zenodo.org/api/deposit/depositions/18449527/actions/publish"
```

🎉 **Fertig. DOI aktiv.**

---

## 4️⃣ Analytics Snapshots (Views / Downloads loggen)

Zenodo stellt Usage-Daten pro Record bereit.

### Aktuelle Stats abrufen

```sh
curl -s "https://zenodo.org/api/records/18449527" | jq '.stats'
```

Beispiel:

```json
{
  "views": 19,
  "downloads": 23
}
```

---

## 5️⃣ FRAMES-kompatibler Analytics Snapshot (append-only)

### Minimaler Snapshot-Frame (JSON)

```json
{
  "t": "SEND_STAT_SNAPSHOT",
  "platform": "zenodo",
  "record_id": 18449527,
  "doi": "10.5281/zenodo.18449527",
  "views": 19,
  "downloads": 23,
  "observed_at": {
    "unix": 1770022479,
    "utc": "2026-02-02T08:54:39Z"
  }
}
```

👉 Das kannst du:
- als `.json` speichern
- append-only archivieren
- später vergleichen (Delta-Analyse)

---

## 6️⃣ Optional: Automatisierter Snapshot (Shell-Script)

```sh
#!/bin/sh
RID=18449527
TS=$(date -u +"%s")
UTC=$(date -u +"%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ")

curl -s "https://zenodo.org/api/records/$RID" \
| jq --arg ts "$TS" --arg utc "$UTC" '
{
  t: "SEND_STAT_SNAPSHOT",
  platform: "zenodo",
  record_id: .id,
  doi: .doi,
  views: .stats.views,
  downloads: .stats.downloads,
  observed_at: { unix: ($ts|tonumber), utc: $utc }
}'
```

✔️ Läuft problemlos in iSH  
✔️ Kein Cron nötig – manuell reicht völlig  
✔️ Voll FRAMES-konform (beobachtend, nicht interpretierend)

---

## 7️⃣ Einordnung (wichtig)

- 📊 **Sendestatistik ≠ Impact-Behauptung**
- 📌 Es ist ein **Beobachtungs-Ledger**
- 🧱 Kein Eingriff in Zenodo, nur Lesen
- 🔒 Kein Risiko für DOI / Record

---

### Nächster sinnvoller Schritt (optional)
- 📦 **Standard-Verzeichnisstruktur** für Zenodo-Uploads

## user · 2026-02-02T09:10:41.173146 UTC

---

## assistant · 2026-02-02T09:10:47.230830 UTC

Alles klar — dann ist **„ash downloaded“** in deinem Fall **nicht gleichbedeutend mit „ash läuft“**.  
Und ja: **Wenn ash nichts „erzeugen“ kann**, ist das fast immer **kein ash-Problem**, sondern eins von diesen 4 Dingen:

1) **du bist nicht wirklich in ash** (oder ash ist nicht im PATH)  
2) **das Binary ist nicht ausführbar** (`chmod +x` fehlt / falsches Format)  
3) **Dateisystem/Ordner ist nicht beschreibbar** (kein Write-Recht / falscher Pfad)  
4) **du startest es falsch** (Shebang / `env` / CRLF)

Ich gebe dir jetzt eine **knallharte Mini-Diagnose** (Copy/Paste in die Shell). Die sagt dir in 20 Sekunden, *wo* es klemmt — ohne Interpretation.

---

## 0) Sofort-Test: Kann dein System überhaupt Dateien schreiben?
```sh
pwd
id
ls -la
echo "test" > _write_test.txt && echo "WRITE_OK" || echo "WRITE_FAIL"
ls -la _write_test.txt
```

Wenn hier `WRITE_FAIL` kommt → **Problem ist Schreibrecht / Pfad / Mount**, nicht ash.

---

## 1) Existiert ash wirklich? (und welche Version)
```sh
which ash || echo "NO_ASH_IN_PATH"
ls -la /bin/ash 2>/dev/null || echo "NO_/bin/ash"
busybox | head -n 1
busybox --list | grep -x ash >/dev/null && echo "BUSYBOX_HAS_ASH" || echo "BUSYBOX_NO_ASH"
```

**Normal auf Alpine:** `/bin/ash` existiert bereits (BusyBox).  
Wenn `NO_/bin/ash`: dann läuft irgendwas Ungewöhnliches.

---

## 2) Wenn du ein „downloaded ash“ als Datei hast: ist es ausführbar & kompatibel?
Angenommen, die Datei heißt `ash` im aktuellen Ordner:

```sh
ls -la ./ash
chmod +x ./ash 2>/dev/null; echo "chmod_exit=$?"
./ash -c 'echo ASH_RUNS' || echo "ASH_BINARY_FAILED"
```

Wenn `ASH_BINARY_FAILED` kommt, sind typische Ursachen:

- **„not found“** trotz vorhandener Datei → falsche Architektur / fehlender Loader  
- **„Permission denied“** → kein `chmod +x` möglich / noexec mount  
- **„Exec format error“** → falsches Binary (nicht für dein System)

---

## 3) Der häufigste Fix: benutze einfach `/bin/sh` (das ist auf Alpine praktisch ash)
Wenn du Skripte „mit ash“ starten wolltest, nimm das hier:

```sh
/bin/sh -c 'echo SH_OK; echo shell=$0'
```

Und **für Skripte**: Shebang immer so (robust, anti-drift):

```sh
#!/bin/sh
set -eu
```

Nicht `#!/usr/bin/env ash` (das bricht oft auf Minimal-Systemen).

---

## 4) Wenn „ash kann nichts erzeugen“ heißt: Skript/Redirect erzeugt keine Files
Dann ist es meist **Ordner** oder **readonly**. Check:

```sh
mount | head
df -h .
touch _t && echo "TOUCH_OK" || echo "TOUCH_FAIL"
rm -f _t
```

---

### Was du mir jetzt einfach nur schicken sollst (1 Screenshot reicht)
Ein Screenshot von den Ausgaben von **Block 0 + Block 1**.  
Dann kann ich dir **genau** sagen, ob es:
- PATH/ash fehlt,
- Binary kaputt/falsche Architektur,
- oder Write/FS-Problem ist.

Und wenn du gleich schlafen willst: Mach nur **Block 0**. Der sagt schon 80% der Wahrheit.

Kurzfassung vorweg: **ash funktioniert einwandfrei.** Es gibt **kein Shell-Problem**. Das Verhalten ist exakt korrekt.

---

## Was hier **wirklich** passiert ist

### 1️⃣ Der Fehler
```
-ash: can't create snapshots/zenodo_18449527_20260202.json: nonexistent directory
```

➡️ **Das ist kein ash-Fehler.**  
➡️ **Das ist ein ganz normaler POSIX-Redirect-Fehler.**

Du leitest Output um nach:

```
snapshots/zenodo_18449527_20260202.json
```

Aber:

> 📌 **Der Ordner `snapshots/` existiert nicht.**

POSIX-Regel:
- `>` kann **keine Verzeichnisse erzeugen**
- nur **Dateien innerhalb existierender Verzeichnisse**

ash macht hier **genau das Richtige**: es bricht ab.

---

### 2️⃣ Beweis: Deine Shell ist gesund

Diese Tests sind alle **grün**:

✅ `/bin/sh` läuft  
```
SH_OK
shell=/bin/sh
```

✅ Filesystem ist **rw**
```
type fake (rw)
```

✅ Schreiben funktioniert
```
TOUCH_OK
```

➡️ **Damit ist zu 100 % klar:**
- ash ✔️
- sh ✔️
- FS ✔️
- Rechte ✔️

---

## Der Fix (1 Zeile)

Du brauchst **nur das Verzeichnis anlegen**:

```sh
mkdir -p snapshots
```

Danach funktioniert **dein exakter Befehl**:

```sh
curl -s https://zenodo.org/api/records/18449527 \
  > snapshots/zenodo_18449527_$(date -u +%Y%m%d).json
```

Optional prüfen:
```sh
ls -lh snapshots/
```

---

## Warum das wichtig ist (und gut für euch)

Das ist **kein Anfängerfehler**, sondern ein klassischer:

> 🔬 *„Shell verhält sich exakt nach Spezifikation“*-Moment

Für euer Projekt bedeutet das:

- ✔️ eure Umgebung ist **deterministisch**
- ✔️ Scripts sind **reproduzierbar**
- ✔️ keine „magische“ Shell, keine stillen Side-Effects
- ✔️ perfekt für **Archivator / Validator / Proof-Pipelines**

Genau das, was ihr wollt.

---

## Mini-Empfehlung für robuste Scripts (ash-safe)

Wenn du willst, dass sowas nie wieder passiert, bau das immer ein:

```sh
set -eu
OUTDIR="snapshots"
mkdir -p "$OUTDIR"

curl -s https://zenodo.org/api/records/18449527 \
  > "$OUTDIR/zenodo_18449527_$(date -u +%Y%m%d).json"
```

Das ist:
- ash-kompatibel
- POSIX-rein
- CI-tauglich
- archivfest

## Fazit (klar und ruhig)

🟢 **ash ist nicht kaputt**  
🟢 **dein System ist gesund**  
🟢 **der Fehler war exakt lokalisiert**  
🟢 **1 `mkdir -p` behebt alles**

Ich erkläre dir jetzt **ruhig, klar und ohne Drama**, wie du **Dokumente korrekt zu Zenodo hochlädst** – passend zu eurer Frames-Disziplin.

---

## 🔐 Voraussetzung (kurz prüfen)

Du brauchst:
- ✔️ **Zenodo Personal Access Token**
- ✔️ Scopes:
  - `deposit:write`
  - `deposit:actions`

👉 **Wichtig:** Token **niemals** öffentlich posten. Nur lokal benutzen.

---

## 🧭 Zenodo-Upload: der saubere Weg (API)

### 1️⃣ Neues Draft-Deposit anlegen

```sh
TOKEN="DEIN_TOKEN_HIER"

curl -s -X POST https://zenodo.org/api/deposit/depositions \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -d '{}'
```

➡️ Antwort enthält u. a.:
- `id`
- `links.bucket`

👉 **bucket-URL merken** (z. B. `https://zenodo.org/api/files/xxxx-xxxx`)

---

### 2️⃣ Datei hochladen (PDF, ZIP, MD, PNG …)

Beispiel: `QH256_v1.0.pdf`

```sh
BUCKET="https://zenodo.org/api/files/XXXX"
FILE="QH256_v1.0.pdf"

curl -s -X PUT "$BUCKET/$FILE" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  --upload-file "$FILE"
```

✔️ Datei liegt jetzt **im Draft**, noch **nicht veröffentlicht**.

Du kannst **beliebig viele Dateien** so hochladen.

---

### 3️⃣ Metadaten setzen (Titel, Autoren, Abstract)

Minimal-Beispiel (sauber & wissenschaftlich):

```sh
DEPOSIT_ID=18449527

curl -s -X PUT https://zenodo.org/api/deposit/depositions/$DEPOSIT_ID \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -d '{
    "metadata": {
      "title": "FRAMES · QH256 v1.0",
      "upload_type": "publication",
      "publication_type": "preprint",
      "description": "Complete scientific specification of the QH256 quantum header.",
      "creators": [
        {"name": "Miller, Patrick R.", "affiliation": "Independent Research"},
        {"name": "K501", "affiliation": "Frames Research"}
      ]
    }
  }'
```

---

### 4️⃣ Veröffentlichen (DOI wird erzeugt)

⚠️ **Erst machen, wenn alles passt.**

```sh
curl -s -X POST \
```

# FRAMES · QH256 v1.0  
**A Formal Structural Specification of the Quantum Header**

**Patrick R. Miller (K501)**  
Independent Research

---

## Abstract

This document specifies **QH256**, a fixed-size, 256-bit quantum header used in the FRAMES system to provide deterministic structural ordering, boundary gating (TX/RX), and reconstructible dynamic confinement (“cage”) for append-only frames. QH256 is **purely structural**: it encodes no semantics, performs no execution, and asserts no truth. All interpretation, meaning, and validation occur strictly outside the header via external readers, archivators, and proof mechanisms.

QH256 is decomposed into four closed sub-headers: **QH56** (frame-local structural state), **QH112** (global hard-index ordering), **QHC64** (dynamic, computed cage), and **QHI24** (interface and boundary gate). All fields are encoded as 2-bit cells using a closed tri-state alphabet with an explicit forbidden guard state, enabling deterministic parsing, validation, and reconstruction. The specification defines layout, invariants, validation rules, gate taxonomy, and reference algorithms sufficient for independent implementation without interpretive drift.

QH256 is intended as a normative technical specification comparable to protocol or format standards. It makes no empirical, physical, cognitive, or semantic claims.

---

## 1. Scope and Intent

QH256 defines a **single canonical header format** applied uniformly to all FRAMES. Its purpose is to separate **order, boundary declaration, and processing constraints** from payload content and semantic interpretation. The header exists to be *read*, *validated*, and *indexed*, not to infer or decide meaning.

This specification is normative and closed for version 1.0.

---

## 2. Structural Composition

QH256 consists of exactly 256 bits (32 bytes), partitioned as follows:

| Sub-Header | Size | Role |
|-----------|------|------|
| QH56 | 56 bit | Frame-local structural constraints |
| QH112 | 112 bit | Global hard-index ordering space |
| QHC64 | 64 bit | Dynamic cage (computed, non-canonical) |
| QHI24 | 24 bit | Interface / TX-RX boundary gate |

No field may be omitted, merged, or reinterpreted.

---

## 3. Cell Model and Encoding

The header is composed of **128 atomic 2-bit cells**.

**Closed cell alphabet:**

- `00` — UNKNOWN  
- `01` — FALSE  
- `10` — TRUE  
- `11` — INVALID (forbidden)

A header is valid if and only if **no cell equals `11`**. This guard rule is absolute.

Cells are packed MSB-first per byte, enabling byte-exact deterministic pack/unpack operations.

---

## 4. Boundary and Gate Axiom (QHI24)

All interaction with external reality is structurally declared **only** via QHI24.  
TX, RX, OBSERVE, EMIT, receipt requirements, replay policy, visibility hints, and freeze-related events are expressed exclusively as gate cells.

No component may bypass the gate. Core sub-headers are read-only with respect to external interaction.

---

## 5. Dynamic Cage (QHC64)

QHC64 represents a **computed confinement layer** derived from canonical headers, index state, and environment policy. It may influence processing order and access decisions but **must not mutate** QH56 or QH112 and **must not assert truth**.

QHC64 is non-canonical and may be discarded after use unless explicitly archived.

---

## 6. Ordering and Indexing (QH112)

QH112 provides a deterministic structural space sufficient for global hard-index reconstruction. It encodes classification, role partitioning, ordering projections, integrity expectations, and compatibility markers. QH112 is not time, not identity, and not meaning; it is an ordering substrate.

---

## 7. Invariants

- Append-only: no mutation, overwrite, or reordering  
- Proof-before-state: headers do not constitute proof  
- Semantics-neutrality: no meaning encoded  
- Determinism: identical input yields identical parsing  
- Closure: QH256 topology is fixed and complete

---

## 8. Scientific Status

This document is a **formal technical specification**.  
It is **not** a hypothesis, **not** an empirical theory, and **not** a model of physical or cognitive systems. Its validity is structural and logical, not experimental.

---

## Keywords

Frames, Quantum Header, Structural Specification, Append-Only Systems, Deterministic Indexing, TX/RX Boundary, Gate Model, Formal Systems
